Braid group action on quantum virtual Grothendieck ring through constructing presentations Thanks: ‡ I.-S. Jang was supported by Incheon National University Research Grant in 2023. Thanks: ⋆ K.-H. Lee was partially supported by a grant from the Simons Foundation (#712100). Thanks: † S.-j. Oh was supported by the Ministry of Education of the Republic of Korea and the National Research Foundation of Korea (NRF-2022R1A2C1004045).
Il-Seung Jang ‡, Kyu-Hwan Lee ⋆, Se-jin Oh †

Abstract
. As a continuation of [ JLO23 ] , we investigate the quantum virtual Grothendieck ring 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) associated with a finite dimensional simple Lie algebra 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} , especially of non-simply-laced type. We establish an isomorphism Ψ Q \Uppsi_{Q} between the heart subring 𝔎 q , Q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}) of 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) associated with a Dynkin quiver Q Q of type 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} and the unipotent quantum coordinate algebra 𝒜 q ​ ( 𝔫 ) \mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n}) of type 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . This isomorphism and the categorification theory via quiver Hecke algebras enable us to obtain a presentation of 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) , which reveals that 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) can be understood as a boson-extension of 𝒜 q ​ ( 𝔫 ) \mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n}) . Then we show that the automorphisms, arising from the reflections on Dynkin quivers and the isomorphisms Ψ Q \Uppsi_{Q} , preserve the canonical basis 𝖫 q \mathsf{L}_{q} of 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) . Finally, we prove that such automorphisms produce a braid group B 𝔤 B_{\mathfrak{g}} action on 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) .



1.
Introduction

The braid group action constructed by Lusztig
[
Lus90a
]
(see also
[
Sai94
,
Kim12
]
) provides essential symmetries for important constructions in the theory of quantum groups.
As we continue our study of the quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
which started in
[
KO23a
,
JLO23
]
, it becomes much desirable to establish a braid group action on
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
when we consider the relationships of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
with the unipotent quantum coordinate algebra and representation theory of quiver Hecke algebras (see (
6.1
) for more detail).
In this paper, we construct such a braid group action by means of presentations of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
coming from the categorification theory via quiver Hecke algebras and various isomorphisms between the heart subrings of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and the unipotent quantum coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
. In what follows, we explain the background and motivation of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, summarize relevant results from
[
JLO23
]
and present the main results of this paper.

Let
q
be an indeterminate. For a complex finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of finite-dimensional modules over the quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{g})
has been intensively
studied due to its rich structure. For instance, it is non-braided as a monoidal category, non-semisimple as an abelian category, and has rigidity.
In
[
CP95a
,
CP95b
]
, Chari and Pressley gave a complete classification of simple modules in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
in terms of Drinfeld polynomials. With the motivation of quantizing
\mathcal{W}
-algebras, Frenkel and Reshetikhin (
[
FR99
]
) introduced the
q
-character for
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, which can be thought as a quantum loop analogue of the natural characters for finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}
-modules.
The
q
-character theory tells us that the Grothendieck ring
K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})

can be understood as a
commutative
polynomial ring generated by the
q
-characters of
fundamental modules

L(Y_{i,p})
’s
((i,p)\in I\times\mathbf{k})
, where
I
is the index set for simple roots of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathbf{k}=\overline{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\subset\bigsqcup_{m>0}\mathbb{C}(\hskip-1.29167pt(q^{1/m})\hskip-1.29167pt)
is the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}(q)
. Since every simple module
L
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
can be obtained as the head of a certain ordered tensor product
M
of fundamental modules
L(Y_{i,p})
in a unique way
[
AK97
,
Kas02
,
VV02
]
, we can label
L
as
L(m)

and
M
as
M(m)
, where
m
is a product of
Y_{i,p}
’s.
Note that the
q
-character of
L(Y_{i,p})
can be computed by a combinatorial algorithm, called Frenkel–Mukhin (FM) algorithm
[
FM01
]
.

Let
t
be another indeterminate. The quantum Grothendieck ring
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}

is a non-commutative
t
-deformation of
K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
in a quantum torus
\mathcal{Y}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
. It was introduced in
[
Nak04
,
VV03
]
for simply-laced types from a geometric motivation, and then in
[
Her04
]
for all types in a uniform way using the inverse of

quantum Cartan matrix
and the
t
-deformed screening operators
[
Her03
]
. The ring
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
has a
canonical basis

\mathbf{L}_{t}
consisting of
(q,t)
-characters
corresponding to simple modules, denoted by
[L]_{t}
for a simple module
L\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, which has the following (conjectural) properties:

(1.1)

(i)

The structure coefficients of
\mathbf{L}_{t}
are positive.

(ii)

[L]_{t}
has positive coefficients.

(iii)

[L]:=[L]_{t}|_{t=1}
recovers the
q
-character of
L
.

Furthermore, the quantum Grothendieck ring admits a quantum loop analogue of Kazhdan–Lusztig theory with respect to
\mathbf{L}_{t}
.
More precisely, Nakajima
[
Nak04
]

established a remarkable Kazhdan–Lusztig type algorithm for simply-laced
\mathfrak{g}
using the geometry of quiver varieties to compute the Jordan–Hölder multiplicity
P_{m,m^{\prime}}
of the simple module
L(m^{\prime})
occurring in
M(m)
. In particular, as a quantum loop analogue of Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials, the polynomials

P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)
are constructed in the quantum Grothendieck ring, and it is proved in
[
Nak04
]
that
P_{m,m^{\prime}}(1)=P_{m,m^{\prime}}
. Since we have

[M(m)]=[L(m)]+\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+};\ m^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m}P_{m,m^{\prime}}[L(m^{\prime})]\quad\text{ in $K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})$},

this result provides a way of computing all the simple
q
-characters (see (
2.9
) for the definition of the partial order
\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
).
Also, Nakajima
[
Nak04
]
and Varagnolo–Vasserot
[
VV03
]
proved that the positivity in (
1.1
) holds for simply-laced types.
For a generalization of these results to non-simply-laced types,
Hernandez
[
Her04
]
formulates
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
as the intersection of the kernels of the
t
-quantized screening operators in
[
Her03
]
(cf.
[
FR99
,
FM01
]
), and then established a (conjectural) Kazhdan–Lusztig type algorithm.
Then it was conjectured in
[
Her04
]
that the natural properties in (
1.1
) also hold for all types.

Recently, Fujita–Hernandez–Oya and the third named author proved a large part of the conjectures for non-simply-laced types by showing the
coincidence of presentations of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
and
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{g})
for a pair
(\mathbf{g},g)
, where the types of
\mathbf{g}
and
g
are given by

(1.2)

(g,\mathbf{g})=(B_{n},A_{2n-1})
,
(C_{n},D_{n+1})
,
(F_{4},E_{6})
and
(G_{2},D_{4})
.

Let us explain this more precisely.
In
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, there exists a skeleton subcategory
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
in the following sense:
Any
prime
simple module in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
can be obtained from a simple module in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
by twisting module actions via a
\mathbf{k}
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{g})
.
Thus, from the representation theoretic viewpoint, it is enough to consider
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
. Compared with
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
,
the quantum Grothendieck ring
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
of
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is contained in the quantum torus
\mathcal{Y}^{0}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
generated by countably many generators, while
\mathcal{Y}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})

is not. In
[
FHOO22
]
, it is proved that

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{g})\simeq\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})\quad\text{as $\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]$-algebras},

and the presentation of
\mathbb{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Q}(t^{1/2})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm 1/2}]}\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})
is known in
[
HL15
]
.
Hence, we conclude that
\mathbb{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{g})
is a
\mathbb{Q}(t^{1/2})
-algebra generated by
\{\mathsf{x}_{i,m}\ |\ i\in I_{\mathbf{g}},\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\}
subject to the relations

(1.4)

\begin{split}&\sum_{s=0}^{1-\mathbf{a}_{i,j}}(-1)^{s}\left[\begin{matrix}1-\mathbf{a}_{i,j}\\
s\end{matrix}\right]_{t}\mathsf{x}_{i,k}^{1-\mathbf{a}_{i,j}-s}\mathsf{x}_{j,k}\mathsf{x}_{i,k}^{s}=0,\\
&\mathsf{x}_{i,k}\mathsf{x}_{j,k+1}=t^{-(\upalpha_{i},\upalpha_{j})}\mathsf{x}_{j,k+1}\mathsf{x}_{i,k}+(1-t^{-(\upalpha_{i},\upalpha_{i})})\delta_{i,j},\\
&\mathsf{x}_{i,k}\mathsf{x}_{j,l}=t^{(-1)^{k+l}(\upalpha_{i},\upalpha_{j})}\mathsf{x}_{j,l}\mathsf{x}_{i,k}\quad\text{ for $l>k+1$},\end{split}

where
(\mathbf{a}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I\mathbf{g}}
is the Cartan matrix of
\mathbf{g}
and
\upalpha_{i}
’s are the simple roots of
\mathbf{g}
. To obtain this presentation, we need to consider the notion of
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
introduced in
[
FO21
]
, which can be understood as a generalization of a Dynkin quiver of simply-laced type.
A
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
Q=(\triangle,\sigma,\xi)
for
\mathfrak{g}
is a triple consisting of (i)
the Dynkin diagram
\triangle(=\triangle_{\mathbf{g}})
of simply-laced type
\mathbf{g}
(need not to be the same as
\triangle_{\mathfrak{g}}
), (ii) the Dynkin diagram automorphism
\sigma
on
\triangle
yielding
\triangle_{\mathfrak{g}}
as the
\sigma
-orbits of
\triangle_{\mathbf{g}}
, and (iii) a height function
\xi:I_{\mathbf{g}}\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
satisfying certain conditions (
[
FO21
, Definition 3.5]
). For each
\mathrm{Q}
-datum
Q

of
\mathfrak{g}
, we can associate a heart monoidal subcategory
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{Q}
of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
.

For any pair of
\mathrm{Q}
-data
Q_{1}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and
Q_{2}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
whose Dynkin diagrams are the
same as
\triangle_{\mathbf{g}}
,
it is proved in
[
FHOO22
, Theorem 8.6]
(cf.
[
HL15
,
HO19
]
) that there exists an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{t}\left(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}_{k}}^{Q_{k}}\right)\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathbf{n})\qquad(k=1,2)

preserving bar involutions on both sides,
where
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathbf{n})
denotes the integral form of the unipotent quantum coordinate algebra of
\mathbf{g}
.
Hence we have

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{t}\left(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}^{Q_{1}}\right)\simeq\mathcal{K}_{t}\left(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}_{2}}^{Q_{2}}\right)

(see
[
FHOO22
, Corollary 10.5, Theorem 10.6]
for more details).
Then using (
1.5
), the rigidity of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
, and singularities of
R
-matrices between fundamental modules, they proved (
1.3
) and established a large part of the conjectures for non-simply-laced
g
, which is referred to as the
propagation of positivity phenomena

[
FHOO22
,
FHOO23
]
.
At this point, for non-simply-laced
g
in (
1.2
), we emphasize that the presentation (
1.4
) of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{g})
depends on the root system of
\mathbf{g}
, and the quantum cluster algebra structure
[
BZ05
]
of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{g})
is still of skew-symmetric type
[
HL16
,
Bit21
,
KKOP21c
,
FHOO23
]
.

To sum up, the representation theory of quantum loop algebras seems to have
simply-laced nature
,
even though
\mathfrak{g}
is of non-simply-laced.
Motivated by this observation, Kashiwara and the third named author introduced in
[
KO23a
]

a new quantum torus
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and the quantum virtual
Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
in
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
for every
\mathfrak{g}
. Note that, for simply-laced type
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
are isomorphic to
\mathcal{Y}^{0}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
, respectively, but not for non-simply-laced types.
For the construction of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, they employed the inverses of
t
-quantized Cartan matrices
and
Dynkin quivers of every finite type
, which seem to have not been well-investigated before. Then it is proved in
[
KO23a
]
that the
q
-commutativity of
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is controlled by the root system of
\mathfrak{g}
, while
\mathcal{Y}^{0}_{t}(g)
is controlled by that of
\mathbf{g}
in (
1.2
)
[
FO21
]
.
Thus the nature of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

does genuinely depend
on the type of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Here we remark that (1) the
(q,t)
-Cartan matrix, introduced by Frenkel–Reshetikhin in
[
FR98
]
,
is a two-parameter deformation of the Cartan matrix of
\mathfrak{g}
, whose specialization at
t=1

is the quantum Cartan matrix, and at
q=1
is the
t
-quantized Cartan matrix, (2)
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is a
q
-deformation of the commutative ring
\mathscr{K}^{-}_{\mathtt{t}}(\mathfrak{g})
, which is the specialization of
the refined ring
\overline{\mathscr{K}}_{{\mspace{1mu}\mathtt{q}\mspace{1mu}},\mathtt{t},\upalpha}(\mathfrak{g})
of interpolating
({\mspace{1mu}\mathtt{q}\mspace{1mu}},\mathtt{t})
-characters in
[
FHR22
]
at
{\mspace{1mu}\mathtt{q}\mspace{1mu}}=1
and
\upalpha=d
, where
\upalpha
is a factor to interpolate several characters (see
[
FHR22
, Remark 6.2(1)]
) and
d
is the lacing number of
\mathfrak{g}
.

In
[
JLO23
]
, the authors of this paper initiated a study of the quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, especially for non-simply-laced types. The results in
[
JLO23
]
are summarized as follows:

(A)

We construct a basis
\mathsf{F}_{q}=\{F_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
in an algorithmic way, whose elements are parameterized by the set
\mathcal{M}_{+}
of dominant monomials in
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Using
\mathsf{F}_{q}
, we construct two other bases

\mathsf{E}_{q}=\{E_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
and
\mathsf{L}_{q}=\{L_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
and show that the transition map between them exhibits the paradigm of Kazhdan–Lusztig theory:

E_{q}(m)=L_{q}(m)+\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+};\ m^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m}P_{m,m^{\prime}}(q)L_{q}(m^{\prime})\quad\text{ for some $P_{m,m^{\prime}}(q)\in q\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q]$}.

We call
\mathsf{L}_{q}
the
canonical basis
and
\mathsf{E}_{q}
the
standard basis
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, respectively.

(B)

\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
has a quantum cluster algebra structure of
skew-symmetrizable type
and the quantum cluster of an initial quantum seed
\mathscr{S}
is a
q
-commuting subset of
\mathsf{F}_{q}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathscr{S}),

where
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathscr{S})
denotes the quantum cluster algebra with an initial quantum seed
\mathscr{S}
.
To prove this, we develop the
quantum folded
T
-systems
among the
Kirillov–Reshetikhin (KR) polynomials

F_{q}(m)
(see Section
3.5
) and employ them as quantum exchange relations of
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathscr{S})
. Then we conclude that every KR-polynomial
F_{q}(m)
appears as a quantum cluster variable.

We also conjecture the following, which is in accordance with one of the ultimate goals of quantum cluster algebra theory that every quantum cluster monomial appears as a dual-canonical/upper-global basis element (see
[
FZ02
, Introduction]
):

Conjecture I

(
[
JLO23
, Conjecture I]
)
.

For each KR-polynomial
F_{q}(m)
, we have

F_{q}(m)=L_{q}(m).

In this paper, we first focus on the heart subring
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
associated with a Dynkin quiver
Q
of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\rm up}
be the normalized dual-canonical/upper-global basis of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
by Lusztig
[
Lus90a
]
and Kashiwara
[
Kas91
,
Kas93
]
(see also
[
Kim12
]
).
We analyze

(a)

the T-system among unipotent quantum minors in the unipotent quantum coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
of
\mathfrak{g}
,

(b)

the quantum folded T-system among KR-polynomials
F_{q}(m)
,

(c)

the quantum cluster algebra structure of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
as a subalgebra of the quantum torus
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
,

and obtain the following theorem:

Theorem A
.

(Theorem
5.2
)

For any Dynkin quiver
Q
of
\mathfrak{g}
, we have an algebra isomorphism

(1.6)

\displaystyle\Psi_{Q}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathfrak{n})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}),

which sends the normalized dual-canonical/upper-global basis
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\rm up}
of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathfrak{n})
to the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q,Q}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{L}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Theorem
A
gives an affirmative answer for the expectation in
[
KO23a
, Remark 6.16]
, which may be regarded as a new realization of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathfrak{n})
in terms of non-commutative Laurent polynomials for non-simply-laced types.
In the proof of Theorem
A
, we also conclude that Conjecture
I
holds when
m
is contained in a quantum subtorus
\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})

for some Dynkin quiver
Q
:

Corollary A
.

(Corollary
5.4
: Partial proof of Conjecture
I
)

Let
m
be a KR-monomial contained in
\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
for some Dynkin quiver
Q
. Then we have

F_{q}(m)=L_{q}(m).

As another byproduct of Theorem
A
combined with a recent work of Qin
[
Qin20
]
(cf.
[
McN21
]
), we have the following result:

Corollary B
.

(Corollary
5.5
)

Every cluster monomial of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
is contained in the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q,Q}
.

Since
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathsf{g})
recovers the quantum Grothendieck ring of the heart subcategory
\mathscr{C}_{\mathsf{g}}^{Q}
when
\mathsf{g}
is of simply-laced finite type, the above result readily follows in that case from
[
HL15
]
(see also
[
HO19
,
FHOO22
]
)
with
[
KKKO18
,
Qin17
]
, so Corollary
B
is an extension of previous results to skew-symmetrizable type cluster algebras.

On the other hand, Theorem
A
tells us that
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
can be understood as a kind of
character ring
of
R^{\mathfrak{g}}\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
from the viewpoint of categorification, where
R^{\mathfrak{g}}\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
is the category of finite-dimensional graded modules over the quiver Hecke algebra
R^{\mathfrak{g}}
.
In
[
KO23b
]
, Kashiwara and the third named author calculated the invariants of
R
-matrices between the cuspidal modules over
R^{\mathfrak{g}}
, where the
cuspidal modules categorify the dual PBW-vectors associated with Dynkin quivers. Based on the results and Theorem
A
, we obtain the presentation of
\mathbb{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/2})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
as follows:

Theorem B
.

(Theorem
7.2
)

The algebra
\mathbb{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is isomorphic to the
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/2})
-algebra generated by

\{\mathsf{y}_{i.m}\ |\ i\in I_{\mathfrak{g}},\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\}
subject to the following relations:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{1-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}}(-1)^{s}\left[\begin{matrix}1-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}\\
s\end{matrix}\right]_{q_{i}}\mathsf{y}_{i,k}^{1-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}-s}\mathsf{y}_{j,k}\mathsf{y}_{i,k}^{s}=0,

\displaystyle\mathsf{y}_{i,k}\mathsf{y}_{j,k+1}=q^{-({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})}\mathsf{y}_{j,k+1}\mathsf{y}_{i,k}+(1-q^{-({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})})\delta_{i,j},

\displaystyle\mathsf{y}_{i,k}\mathsf{y}_{j,l}=q^{(-1)^{k+l}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})}\mathsf{y}_{j,l}\mathsf{y}_{i,k}\quad\text{ for $l>k+1$},

where
\{{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\ |\ i\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}\}
denotes the set of simple roots of
\mathfrak{g}
,

q_{i}\mathbin{:=}q^{({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})/2}
and
(\mathsf{c}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}}
denotes the Cartan matrix of
\mathfrak{g}
. We denote by

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
the
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/2})
-algebra with presentation in (
1.7
).

Note that we can obtain a presentation with respect to any Dynkin quiver
Q
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then using Dynkin quivers
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, we can induce an automorphism
\widetilde{\Uptheta}(Q^{\prime},Q)
on
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and hence on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
:

Theorem C
.

(Theorem
7.3
)
For an arbitrary pair of Dynkin quivers
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, we have an automorphism
\widetilde{\Uptheta}(Q^{\prime},Q)
on
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, which makes the diagram below commutative and preserves
the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q}
.

\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Uptheta}(Q^{\prime},Q)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(\mathfrak{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{Q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}

Let us choose a pair of Dynkin quivers, consisting of a Dynkin quiver
Q
with a source at
i
and its reflected Dynkin quiver
s_{i}Q
,
and set
\textbf{{T}}_{i}\mathbin{:=}\widetilde{\Uptheta}(s_{i}Q,Q)
. Concentrating on such a pair, we prove that

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
is a module over the Braid group
B_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

Theorem D
.

(Theorem
8.1
)
The automorphisms
\textbf{{T}}^{\pm}_{i}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
can be described as follows:

(1.8)

\displaystyle\textbf{{T}}_{i}(\mathsf{y}_{j,m})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{y}_{j,m+\delta_{i,j}}&\text{if}\ \mathsf{c}_{i,j}\geqslant 0,\\[4.30554pt]
\dfrac{\left(\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}}(-1)^{k}q_{i}^{-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}/2-k}\mathsf{y}_{i,m}^{(k)}\mathsf{y}_{j,m}\mathsf{y}_{i,m}^{(-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}-k)}\right)}{(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})^{-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}}}&\text{if}\ \mathsf{c}_{i,j}<0,\end{cases}

and

(1.9)

\displaystyle\textbf{{T}}_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{y}_{j,m})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{y}_{j,m-\delta_{i,j}}&\text{if}\ \mathsf{c}_{i,j}\geqslant 0,\\[4.30554pt]
\dfrac{\left(\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}}(-1)^{k}q_{i}^{-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}/2-k}\mathsf{y}_{i,m}^{(-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}-k)}\mathsf{y}_{j,m}\mathsf{y}_{i,m}^{(k)}\right)}{(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})^{-\mathsf{c}_{i,j}}}&\text{if}\ \mathsf{c}_{i,j}<0,\end{cases}

where

\mathsf{y}_{i,m}^{(k)}\mathbin{:=}\dfrac{\mathsf{y}_{i,m}^{k}}{[k]_{q_{i}}!}.

Furthermore,

the set of automorphisms
\{\textbf{{T}}^{\pm}_{i}\ |\ i\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}\}
induces a Braid group
B_{\mathfrak{g}}
action on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
.

.

Note that

(a)

Theorem
D
can be understood as a generalization the
B_{\mathfrak{g}}
-action on the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of Lusztig and
Saito
[
Lus90b
,
Sai94
]
to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
,

(b)

Theorem
D
and its proof for simply-laced
\mathfrak{g}
are announced in
[
KKOP21a
]
.

Let us explain the argument of the proof for Theorem
D
.
First of all, we obtain the formulae from Theorem
B
and Theorem
C
by comparing the presentations corresponding to
Q
and
s_{i}Q
.
An attempt to prove Theorem
D
by directly applying the formulae in (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) brings out very complicated computations, which does not seem feasible by hand for non-simply-laced types, because we need to check that (i)
\{\textbf{{T}}_{i}(\mathsf{y}_{j,m})\}_{j\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}}
satisfy the relations in (
1.7
) and (ii)
\{\textbf{{T}}_{i}\}_{i\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}}
satisfy the braid relations, such as
\textbf{{T}}_{i}\textbf{{T}}_{j}\textbf{{T}}_{i}(\mathsf{y}_{k,m})=\textbf{{T}}_{j}\textbf{{T}}_{i}\textbf{{T}}_{j}(\mathsf{y}_{k,m})
and
\textbf{{T}}_{i}\textbf{{T}}_{j}\textbf{{T}}_{i}\textbf{{T}}_{j}(\mathsf{y}_{k,m})=\textbf{{T}}_{j}\textbf{{T}}_{i}\textbf{{T}}_{j}\textbf{{T}}_{i}(\mathsf{y}_{k,m})
for all
(k,m)\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, as operators on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
. Instead, for each Dynkin quiver
Q
,
we understand
\mathsf{y}_{j,0}
as an image of dual PBW vector of weight
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j}
in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
through the isomorphism
\widetilde{\Uptheta}_{Q}

between
\mathbb{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
(Theorem
7.2
), and
\mathsf{y}_{j,m}
as the
m
-th dual
of the image. Then using the isomorphisms
\widetilde{\Uptheta}_{Q}
and
\widetilde{\Uptheta}_{s_{i}Q}
, the combinatorics of AR-quivers
\Gamma^{Q}
and
\Gamma^{s_{i}Q}
, and relations among dual PBW vectors in
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
, we prove
Theorem
D
without complicated direct computations.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we review the definition of the quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and its properties. In particular, we emphasize the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. In Section
3
, we first recall various quivers such as Dynkin quivers, AR-quivers,
and repetition quivers, which play important roles in the study of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then we define subrings of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, whose bases are compatible with the bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. We also investigate the multiplicative structure of
F_{q}(Y_{i,p})
in terms of the notion of

Q
-weight, which will be used in later sections. In Section
4
, we review materials on the unipotent quantum coordinate algebra, especially their elements called the unipotent quantum minors,
the dual-canonical/upper-global basis, and the dual PBW basis. In Section
5
, we prove Theorem
A
by applying the framework of
[
HL15
,
HO19
]
due to
Hernadez–Leclerc–Oya. In Section
6
, we review the categorification theory of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
via quiver Hecke algebras
and investigate the interrelationship between the categorification and the isomorphism
\Psi_{Q}
in Theorem
A
. In Section
7
, based on the results in the previous sections, we prove Theorem
B

(see also Proposition
6.12
and Corollary
6.15
). Then we prove Theorem
C
by adopting the methods in
[
HL15
,
FHOO22
]
. In Section
8
, we prove Theorem
D
, which is the culminating result of this paper. To prove this theorem, we use the equations between dual PBW vectors in
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
and
F_{q}(Y_{i,p})
’s
in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, circumventing direct calculations involving the expressions in Theorem
D
. Still, a large part of this paper is inevitably devoted to explicit computations for the proof of Theorem
D
.

Convention

Throughout this paper, we use the following convention.

\bullet

For a statement
\mathtt{P}
, we set
\delta(\mathtt{P})
to be
1
or
0

depending on whether
\mathtt{P}
is true or not.

\bullet

For
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
, we write
k\equiv_{s}l
if
s
divides
k-l
and
k\not\equiv_{s}l
, otherwise.

\bullet

For a totally ordered set
J=\{\cdots<j_{-1}<j_{0}<j_{1}<j_{2}\cdots\}
, write

\prod_{j\in J}^{\to}A_{j}\mathbin{:=}\cdots A_{j_{-1}}A_{j_{0}}A_{j_{1}}A_{j_{2}}\cdots.

2.
Quantum virtual Grothendieck rings

In this section, we briefly review the definition of quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
by following
[
JLO23
]
. We emphasize here that

\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is isomorphic to the quantum Grothendieck ring of quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{g})
when
\mathfrak{g}
is of simply-laced finite type.

2.1.
Cartan datum

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra of rank
n
. We denote by
I=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
the index set, by
\mathsf{C}=(\mathsf{c}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}

the Cartan matrix of
\mathfrak{g}
, by
\Pi=\{{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the set of simple roots, by
\Pi^{\vee}=\{h_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the set of simple coroots and by
\mathsf{P}
the weight lattice of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Note that there exists a diagonal matrix
\mathsf{D}={\rm diag}(d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}\ |\ i\in I)
such that

\overline{\mathsf{B}}=\mathsf{D}\mathsf{C}\text{ and }\underline{\mathsf{B}}=\mathsf{C}\mathsf{D}^{-1}\text{ are symmetric}.

We take such
\mathsf{D}
satisfying
\min(d_{i}\ |\ i\in I)=1
throughout this paper. We have the symmetric bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)
on
\mathsf{P}
such that

({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})=d_{i}\mathsf{c}_{i,j}=d_{j}\mathsf{c}_{j,i}\quad\text{ and }\quad\langle h_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j}\rangle=\dfrac{2({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})}{({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})}.

For
i,j\in I
, we set

(2.1)

\displaystyle h_{i,j}\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }\mathsf{c}_{i,j}\mathsf{c}_{j,i}=0,\\
3&\text{ if }\mathsf{c}_{i,j}\mathsf{c}_{j,i}=1,\\
4&\text{ if }\mathsf{c}_{i,j}\mathsf{c}_{j,i}=2,\\
6&\text{ if }\mathsf{c}_{i,j}\mathsf{c}_{j,i}=3.\end{cases}

We denote by
\Phi_{\pm}
the
set of positive
(
resp. negative
)
roots of
\mathfrak{g}
.

For
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi_{+}
, we set
d_{{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}}\mathbin{:=}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha})/2\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
.
For each
i\in I
, we write
\varpi_{i}
the
fundamental weight
of
\mathsf{P}
given by
\langle h_{i},\varpi_{i}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
and set
\rho\mathbin{:=}\sum_{i\in I}\varpi_{i}
.
The free abelian group
\mathsf{Q}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}
is called the
root lattice
.
We set
\mathsf{Q}^{\pm}\mathbin{:=}\pm\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\subset\mathsf{P}^{\pm}\mathbin{:=}\pm\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}\varpi_{i}
. For
\mu,\zeta\in\mathsf{P}
, we write
\mu\preccurlyeq\zeta
if
\mu-\zeta\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
.
For
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}=\sum_{i\in I}k_{i}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
, we set
{\rm ht}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\mathbin{:=}\sum_{i\in I}k_{i}
and

{\rm supp}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\mathbin{:=}\{i\in I\ |\ k_{i}\neq 0\}
.
For
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\in\Phi_{+}
, the
multiplicity

{\rm mul}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
is defined by

{\rm mul}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\mathbin{:=}\max\{n_{i}\,\mid\,i\in I\}
.
For
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\Phi
, we set

p_{{\mspace{1mu}\beta},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}}\mathbin{:=}\max\{p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ {\mspace{1mu}\beta}-p{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi\}

(see (
4.15
) below).

Slightly modifying the usual notation of
Dynkin diagram
, we use the following convention of Dynkin diagram
\triangle
,
consisting of the set of vertices
\triangle_{0}=I
and the set of edges
\triangle_{1}
, as follows:

(2.2)

\displaystyle A_{n}\ \ \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.83623pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.83623pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-4.83623pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to9.67pt{\vbox to9.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83623pt\lower-4.83623pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

Here
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.37675pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.37675pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-4.37675pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to8.75pt{\vbox to8.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.37675pt\lower-4.37675pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

means that
({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{k},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{k})=t
. We write
i\sim j
for
i,j\in I
if
\mathsf{c}_{i,j}<0
. We denote by

d(i,j)
the number of edges between
i
and
j
in
\triangle
. For instance,
d(i,j)=1
if
i\sim j
.

2.2.
Weyl group
\mathsf{W}
, reduced sequences and convex orders

Let us set up notations and briefly summarize some results associated with the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
, which can be found in
[
Bou02
,
Hum90
]
.
We denote by
\mathsf{W}
the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
and
s_{i}
the simple reflection of
\mathsf{W}
for each
i\in I
. A
Coxeter element
of
\mathsf{W}
is a product of the
form
s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{n}}
such that
\{i_{k}\ |\ 1\leqslant k\leqslant n\}=I
. Note that all Coxeter elements are conjugate and the order of Coxeter elements is known as
Coxeter number
\mathsf{h}
.
We denote by
w_{\circ}
the longest element in
\mathsf{W}
. Recall that
w_{\circ}
induces an involution
{}^{*}:I\to I
given by
w_{\circ}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})=-{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i^{*}}
.

For an element
w\in\mathsf{W}
, we write
\ell(w)
for its length. A sequence
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\in I^{l}
is said to be
reduced
if

s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
is a reduced expression of an element
w\in\mathsf{W}
.
We denote by
I(w)
the set of all reduced sequences of
w\in\mathsf{W}
.
Two reduced sequences
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{l})
and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{l})
are said to be
commutation equivalent
if
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
can be obtained from

i
by transforming some adjacent components
(i,j)
into
(j,i)
when
i\not\sim j
.
In this case, we denote by
\textbf{{i}}\overset{c}{\sim}\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
.
We denote
[\textbf{{i}}]
the equivalent class of
i

with respect to
\overset{c}{\sim}
, and we often call it the
commutation class
of
i
.

Let us consider a reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})
in
I(w_{\circ})
. Note that each
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}
induces a
convex total order
on
\Phi_{+}
in the following way (cf.
[
Pap94
]
). Setting
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{k}^{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}\mathbin{:=}s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i_{k}})
,
we have
\{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{k}^{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}\ |\ 1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell\}=\Phi_{+}
with
|\Phi_{+}|=\ell
and
the total order defined by
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{k}^{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}\leqslant_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{l}^{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}\iff k\leqslant l
satisfies the following property: For
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\Phi_{+}
with

{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}+{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\Phi_{+}
, we have either
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}<_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}+{\mspace{1mu}\beta}<_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
or

{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\leqslant_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}+{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\leqslant_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
. One step further, for a commutation class
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
, we can define the
convex partial order

\prec_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}
on
\Phi_{+}
by

{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\prec_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
if and only if
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\leqslant_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
for all
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}\in[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
.

For a commutation class
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
of
w_{\circ}
and
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi_{+}
, we define
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-residue of
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
, denoted by

\mathrm{res}^{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha})
to be
i_{k}\in I
such that
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}_{k}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
. This is well-defined; that is, for any
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell})\in[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
with
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}}_{l}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
, we have
i_{k}=j_{l}
.
For a reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{\ell})
of
w_{\circ}
, it is known that the expression
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}^{\prime}\mathbin{:=}(i_{2},\cdots,i_{\ell},i_{1}^{*})
is also a reduced sequence
of
w_{\circ}
. This operation is sometimes referred to as the
combinatorial reflection functor
and we write
r_{i_{1}}\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}=\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}
. Also it induces the operation on commutation classes of
w_{\circ}
(i.e.,
r_{i_{1}}[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]=[r_{i_{1}}\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]

is well-defined if there exists a reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}^{\prime}=(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{\ell})\in[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
such that
j_{1}=i_{1}
).

2.3.
Exponents

An element
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}=\bm{(}{\textbf{{e}}_{\beta}}\bm{)}
of
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{\Phi_{+}}
is called an
exponent
.
For an exponent
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}
, we set
{\rm wt}(\underline{\textbf{{e}}})\mathbin{:=}\sum_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}\textbf{{e}}_{\beta}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
.

Definition 2.1

(cf.
[
McN15
,
Oh19
]
)
.

For a reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})
and a commutation class
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
,
we define the partial orders
<^{\mathtt{b}}_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}
and
\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}
on
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{\Phi_{+}}
as follows:

(i)

<^{\mathtt{b}}_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}
is the bi-lexicographical partial order induced by
<_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}
. Namely,
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}<^{\mathtt{b}}_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}\underline{\textbf{{e}}}^{\prime}
if

•

{\rm wt}(\underline{\textbf{{e}}})={\rm wt}(\underline{\textbf{{e}}}^{\prime})
,

•

there exists
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi^{+}
such that

\textbf{{e}}_{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}<\textbf{{e}}^{\prime}_{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
and
\textbf{{e}}_{\mspace{1mu}\beta}=\textbf{{e}}^{\prime}_{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
for any
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
such that
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}<_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
,

•

there exists
\eta\in\Phi^{+}
such that

\textbf{{e}}_{\eta}<\textbf{{e}}^{\prime}_{\eta}
and
\textbf{{e}}_{\zeta}=\textbf{{e}}^{\prime}_{\zeta}
for any
\zeta
such that
\eta<_{\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}}\zeta
.

(ii)

For exponents
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}
and
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}^{\prime}
, we define
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}\underline{\textbf{{e}}}^{\prime}
if the following conditions are satisfied:

\underline{\textbf{{e}}}<^{\mathtt{b}}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}}\underline{\textbf{{e}}}^{\prime}\quad\text{for all $\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}_{\circ}\in[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]$.}

We say that an exponent
\underline{\textbf{{e}}}=\bm{(}{\textbf{{e}}_{\mspace{1mu}\beta}}\bm{)}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{\Phi^{+}}
is
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-simple
if it is minimal with respect to the partial order
\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}
. For a given
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-simple exponent

\underline{\textbf{{s}}}=\bm{(}{\textbf{{s}}_{\beta}}\bm{)}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{\Phi^{+}}
, we call a cover
1
1

1

Recall that a
cover
of
x
in a poset
P
with partial order
\prec
is an element
y\in P
such that
x\prec y
and there
does not exists
y^{\prime}\in P
such that
x\prec y^{\prime}\prec y
.

of
\underline{\textbf{{s}}}
under
\prec^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}
a
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-minimal exponent for
\underline{\textbf{{s}}}
.
A pair
\langle\hskip-2.58334pt\langle{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\rangle\hskip-2.58334pt\rangle
is called a
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-pair
if

{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\Phi^{+}
satisfy
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\not\preccurlyeq^{\mathtt{b}}_{[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}
.
We regard a
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-pair
{\underline{\textbf{{p}}}}\mathbin{:=}\langle\hskip-2.58334pt\langle{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\rangle\hskip-2.58334pt\rangle
as an exponent
by
\textbf{{p}}_{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}=\textbf{{p}}_{\mspace{1mu}\beta}=1
and
\textbf{{p}}_{\gamma}=0
for

\gamma\not={\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
.

Note that, for any
\gamma\in\Phi_{+}\setminus\Pi
and
\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}\in I(w_{\circ})
, there exist a
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-minimal
[\textbf{{i}}_{\circ}]
-pair
\langle\hskip-2.58334pt\langle{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\rangle\hskip-2.58334pt\rangle
such that
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}+{\mspace{1mu}\beta}=\gamma
.

2.4.
Quantum virtual Grothendieck rings via
t
-quantized Cartan matrices

For
i,j\in I
, we set

\underline{\mathsf{c}}_{i,j}(t)\mathbin{:=}\delta(i=j)(t+t^{-1})+\delta(i\neq j)\mathsf{c}_{i,j}

and call the matrix
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)\mathbin{:=}(\underline{\mathsf{c}}_{i,j}(t))_{i,j\in I}
the
t
-quantized Cartan matrix
. Then

\underline{\mathsf{B}}(t)\mathbin{:=}\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)\mathsf{D}^{-1}\text{ is a symmetric matrix in ${\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm 1}])$}.

Regarding
\underline{\mathsf{B}}(t)
as a
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-valued matrix, the matrix
\underline{\mathsf{B}}(t)
is invertible and we denote its inverse by
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}(t)=(\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t))_{i,j\in I}
. We write

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)t^{u}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}(\!(q)\!)

for the Laurent expansion of the
(i,j)
-entry
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)
at
t=0
. Note that
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)=\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{j,i}(u)
for
i,j\in I
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
HL15
,
FO21
,
FM23
,
KO23a
]
)
.

For any
i,j\in I
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, we have

(1)

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)=0
if
u\leqslant d(i,j)
or
d(i,j)\equiv_{2}u
,

(2)

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(d(i,j)+1)=\max(d_{i},d_{j})
,

(3)

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u+\mathsf{h})=\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j^{*}}(u)
and
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u+2\mathsf{h})=\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)
for
u\geqslant 0
,

(4)

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(\mathsf{h}-u)=\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j^{*}}(u)
for
0\leqslant u\leqslant\mathsf{h}
and
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(2\mathsf{h}-u)=-\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)
for
0\leqslant u\leqslant 2\mathsf{h}
,

(5)

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)\geqslant 0
for
0\leqslant u\leqslant\mathsf{h}
and
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)\leqslant 0
for
\mathsf{h}\leqslant u\leqslant 2\mathsf{h}
.

As seen in Lemma
2.2
,
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)
is periodic. Thus it is enough to compute

(2.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{i,j}(t)\mathbin{:=}\sum_{u=0}^{\mathsf{h}-1}\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)t^{u}\quad\text{ for each }i,j\in I.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{i,j}(t)
’s are completely calculated (see
[
HL15
,
FO21
,
KO23a
]
). We exhibit
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)
of non-simply-laced types only:

Proposition 2.3
.

For
i,j\in I
, the closed-form formula of
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)

(i\leqslant j)
is given as follows:

(1)

For
\triangle
of type
B_{n}
or
C_{n}
with their Coxeter number
\mathsf{h}=2n
, we have

(2.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{i,j}(t)=\begin{cases}\max(d_{i},d_{j})\sum_{s=1}^{i}t^{n-i-1+2s}&\text{ if }i\leqslant j=n,\\
\max(d_{i},d_{j})\sum_{s=1}^{i}(t^{j-i+2s-1}+t^{2n-j-i+2s-1})&\text{ if }i\leqslant j<n.\end{cases}

(2)

For
\triangle
of type
F_{4}
with its Coxeter number
\mathsf{h}=12
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{1,1}(t)=2(t+t^{5}+t^{7}+t^{11}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{1,2}(t)=2(t^{2}+t^{4}+2t^{6}+t^{8}+t^{10}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{1,3}(t)=2(t^{3}+t^{5}+t^{7}+t^{9}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{1,4}(t)=2(t^{4}+t^{8}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{2,2}(t)=2(t+2t^{3}+3t^{5}+3t^{7}+2t^{9}+t^{11}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{2,3}(t)=2(t^{2}+2t^{4}+2t^{6}+2t^{8}+t^{10}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{2,4}(t)=2(t^{3}+t^{5}+t^{7}+t^{9}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{3,3}(t)=t+2t^{3}+3t^{5}+3t^{7}+2t^{9}+t^{11},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{3,4}(t)=t^{2}+t^{4}+2t^{6}+t^{8}+t^{10},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{4,4}(t)=t+t^{5}+t^{7}+t^{11}.

(3)

For
\triangle
of type
G_{2}
with its Coxeter number
\mathsf{h}=6
, we have

\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{1,1}(t)=t+2t^{3}+t,\qquad\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{1,2}(t)=3(t^{2}+t^{4}),\qquad\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{2,2}(t)=3(t+2t^{3}+t).

For
i,j\in I
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, we set

(2.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{i,j}[k]\mathbin{:=}\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(k-1).

Based on Lemma
2.2
(
1
), we fix a parity function
\epsilon:I\to\{0,1\}
such that
\epsilon_{i}\neq\epsilon_{j}
for
i\sim j
. We define a subset
{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}

of
I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
as follows:

(2.6)

\displaystyle{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}=\{(i,p)\in I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ p\equiv_{2}\epsilon_{i}\}.

Let
q
be an indeterminate with a formal square root
q^{1/2}
. For
i\in I
, we set
q_{i}\mathbin{:=}q^{d_{i}}
.

Definition 2.4

(
[
Nak04
,
Her04
,
VV03
,
KO23a
]
)
.

We define a quantum torus
(\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}),*)
to be the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-algebra generated by
\{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm}\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
subject to following relations:

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}=\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{j,s}=q^{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)}\widetilde{X}_{j,s}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p},

where

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)=\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(p-s-1)-\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(s-p-1)-\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(p-s+1)+\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(s-p+1).

If there is no danger of confusion, we write
\mathcal{X}_{q}
instead of
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.
Note that
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{j,p}=\widetilde{X}_{j,p}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
for
(i,p),(j,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
by Lemma
2.2
.

There exists a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-algebra anti-involution
\overline{(\cdot)}
on
\mathcal{X}_{q}
(
[
Her04
,
KO23a
]
) given by

(2.7)

\displaystyle q^{1/2}\longmapsto q^{-1/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\longmapsto q_{i}\widetilde{X}_{i,p}.

We say that
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
is a monomial if it is a product of generators
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm}
and
q^{\pm 1/2}
; i,e;

(2.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}=q^{a}\prod_{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}^{\to}\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})}\quad\text{ for some $a\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}$ and $u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}$.}

A monomial
\widetilde{m}
is called
dominant
if
it is a product of generators
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
(not
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}
) and
q^{\pm 1/2}
; equivalently
u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}
in (
2.8
) for all
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.
A monomial
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
is said to be

commutative
if
\widetilde{m}=\overline{\widetilde{m}}
.
We denote by

\mathcal{X}
the commutative Laurent polynomial ring obtained by specializing
q
at
1
to
\mathcal{X}_{q}
. We write the map
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}:\mathcal{X}_{q}\to\mathcal{X}
and set
X_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(q^{a}\widetilde{X}_{i,p})
for any
a\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
.
Then we have

\mathcal{X}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[X_{i,p}^{\pm 1}\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}].

Remark 2.5
.

To a monomial
m\in\mathcal{X}
, we can associate the commutative monomial
\underline{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}

such that
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\underline{m})=m
. Hereafter, we use
\underline{m}
for commutative monomials in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
.

We set
\mathcal{M}
the set of all monomials in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
the subset of dominant monomials in
\mathcal{M}
.
For a monomial
m\in\mathcal{M}
, we set

{\rm range}(m)\mathbin{:=}[a,b],

where

\text{$a=\min(p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ X_{i,p}^{\pm 1}\text{ is a factor of $m$})$ and
$b=\max(p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ X_{i,p}^{\pm 1}\text{ is a factor of $m$})$}.

For
m\in\mathcal{X}
with
{\rm range}(m)=[a,b]
, the monomial
m
is said to be
right-negative
if
X_{i,b}
has negative power for every factor
X_{i,b}
of
m
(
i\in I
).

For an element
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we denote by
\mathcal{M}(x)
the set of all monomials appearing in
x
. We set

{\rm range}(x)\mathbin{:=}\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}(x)}{\rm range}(m).

Finally, for a monomial
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
and an element
\widetilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we set

{\rm range}(\widetilde{m})\mathbin{:=}{\rm range}({\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m}))\quad\text{ and }\quad{\rm range}(\widetilde{x})\mathbin{:=}\displaystyle\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\cup$}}\limits_{\widetilde{m}:\text{monomial in $\widetilde{x}$}}{\rm range}(\widetilde{m}).

For monomials
\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we set

\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\mathbin{:=}\sum_{(i,p),(j,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})u_{j,s}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s).

For commutative monomials
\underline{m},\underline{m}^{\prime}
, the monomial

\underline{m}\cdot\underline{m}^{\prime}\mathbin{:=}q^{-\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(\underline{m},\underline{m}^{\prime})/2}\underline{\widetilde{m}}*\underline{\widetilde{m}}^{\prime}\text{ is commutative again.}

For each
(i,p)\in I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
with
(i,p-1)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, we define the commutative monomial
\widetilde{B}_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}\underline{B_{i,p}}
where

B_{i,p}=X_{i,p-1}X_{i,p+1}\prod_{j\sim i}X_{j,p}^{\mathsf{c}_{j,i}}\in\mathcal{X}.

For each
i\in I
, we denote by
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})
the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-algebra of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by

\widetilde{X}_{i,l}*(1+q_{i}^{-1}\widetilde{B}^{-1}_{i,l+1})\text{ and }\widetilde{X}_{j,s}^{\pm 1}\quad\text{ for }j\in I\setminus\{0\}\text{ and }(i,p),(j,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}.

Definition 2.6

(
[
Her04
,
JLO23
]
)
.

We set

\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\mathbin{:=}\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})

and call it the
quantum virtual Grothendieck ring associated to
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
. In particular, it is known as

quantum Grothendieck ring

when
\mathfrak{g}
is of simply-laced type.

If there is no danger of confusion, we write
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
instead of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

2.5.
Bases

For monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, we write

(2.9)

\displaystyle m\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m^{\prime}\text{ if and only if }m^{-1}m^{\prime}\text{ is a product of elements in }\{B_{i,p}\ |\ (i,p+1)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}.

This defines the
Nakajima partial order
on
\mathcal{M}
.
For monomials
\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we write
\widetilde{m}\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\widetilde{m}^{\prime}
if
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})
.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
Her04
,
JLO23
]
, cf.
[
FM01
]
)
.

For each dominant monomial
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, there exists a unique element
F_{q}(m)
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
such that

\underline{m}
is the unique dominant monomial appearing in
F_{q}(m)

and
\overline{F_{q}(m)}=F_{q}(m)
.

Furthermore,

(a)

a monomial appearing in
F_{q}(m)-\underline{m}
is strictly less than
\underline{m}
with respect to
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
;

(b)

\mathsf{F}_{q}\mathbin{:=}\{F_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
forms a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
;

(c)

\mathfrak{K}_{q}
is generated by the set
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
as a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-algebra;

(d)

all
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomials of
F_{q}(X_{i,p})-\underline{X_{i,p}}-\underline{X_{i^{*},p+\mathsf{h}}}
are product of
\widetilde{X}_{j,u}^{\pm 1}
with
p<u<p+\mathsf{h}
.

For
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we set

E_{q}(m)\mathbin{:=}q^{b}\left(\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits^{\to}_{p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}\left(\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits_{i;\in I,(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}F_{q}(X_{i,p})^{u_{i,p}(m)}\right)\right),

where
b
is an element in
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that
\underline{m}
appears in
E_{q}(m)
with coefficient
1
.
We remark here that, for a fixed
p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, the set
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
forms a commuting family so that
E_{q}(m)
is well-defined.
Then the set
\mathsf{E}_{q}\mathbin{:=}\{E_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}\}
also forms a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
and satisfies

(2.10)

\displaystyle E_{q}(m)=F_{q}(m)+\sum_{m^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m}C_{m,m^{\prime}}F_{q}(m^{\prime})

for some
C_{m,m^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
.

Using the basis
\mathsf{F}_{q}
and
\mathsf{E}_{q}
, we construct a basis
\mathsf{L}_{q}
, called the
canonical basis
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
:

Theorem 2.8

(
[
Nak04
,
Her04
,
JLO23
]
)
.

For each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, there exists a unique element
L_{q}(m)
in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
such that

(2.11)

(i)

\overline{L_{q}(m)}=L_{q}(m)
and

(ii)

L_{q}(m)=E_{q}(m)+\sum_{m^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m}Q_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)E_{q}(m^{\prime})
for some
Q_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)\in q\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q]
.

Furthermore,

(a)

a monomial appearing in
L_{q}(m)-\underline{m}
is strictly less than
\underline{m}
with respect to
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
;

(b)

\mathsf{L}_{q}\mathbin{:=}\{L_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}\}
forms a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
;

(c)

L_{q}(X_{i,p})=F_{q}(X_{i,p})
for all
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

Note that, for
r\in 2\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, we have a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-algebra endomorphism on
\mathcal{X}_{q}

(2.12)

\mathscr{S}_{r}:\mathcal{X}_{q}\to\mathcal{X}_{q}\quad\text{ given by }\quad\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\longmapsto\widetilde{X}_{i,p+r}

which induces a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-algebra endomorphism on
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
such that

\mathscr{S}_{r}(F_{q}(X_{i,p}))=F_{q}(X_{i,p+r}).

We call it the
parameter shift by
r
.

3.
Quivers and subrings

In this section, we introduce various quivers such as Dynkin quivers, repetition quivers, and Auslander–Reiten (AR) quivers, which will be used for defining subrings of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
.
Then we review the notion of
Q
-weights and investigate the multiplicative relations among
F_{q}(Y_{i,p})
’s by using their
Q
-weights.
At the end of this section, we also review the quantum folded
T
-system in terms of
F_{q}(m)
with KR-monomials
m
and the truncation homomorphisms.

3.1.
Dynkin quivers

A Dynkin quiver
Q
is a pair
(\triangle,\xi)
consisting of (i) a Dynkin diagram
\triangle
and (ii) a height function
\xi:\triangle_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
satisfying

|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|=1
if
i\sim j
. For a given Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we can associate an oriented graph whose underlying graph is
\triangle
and arrows between
i
and
j
are assigned as follows:

i\to j\quad\text{ if }i\sim j\text{ and }\xi_{i}=\xi_{j}+1.

Conversely, for a given oriented graph on
\triangle
, we can associate a Dynkin quiver whose height function is well-defined up to integers (see
[
KO23a
]
for more details).

For a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we call
i\in\triangle_{0}
a
source
(resp.
sink
) of
Q
if
\xi_{i}>\xi_{j}
(resp.
\xi_{i}<\xi_{j}
) for all
j\in\triangle_{0}
with
j\sim i
.
For a Dynkin quiver
Q
and its source
i
, we denote by
s_{i}Q=(\triangle,s_{i}\xi)
a Dynkin quiver on
\triangle
whose height function
s_{i}\xi
is defined as follows:

(s_{i}\xi)_{j}\mathbin{:=}\xi_{j}-\delta(i=j)2.

Note that
i
is a sink of the Dynkin quiver
s_{i}Q
.

Remark 3.1
.

Recall that we fixed the parity function
\epsilon
in Section
2.4
. Throughout this paper, we only consider Dynkin quivers
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
such that

\xi_{i}\equiv_{2}\epsilon_{i}
for all
i\in\triangle_{0}
.

For a sequence
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\in I^{l}
, we say
i
is
adapted to
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
if

\text{ $i_{k}$ is a source of $s_{i_{k-1}}s_{i_{k-2}}\cdots s_{i_{1}}Q$ for all $1\leqslant k\leqslant l$}.

For instance, a sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
, for which
\xi_{i_{1}}\geqslant\xi_{i_{2}}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\xi_{i_{n}}
and
\{i_{k}\ |\ 1\leqslant k\leqslant n\}=I
is a reduced sequence of a Coxeter element
\tau
, is adapted to
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
(see
[
FO21
,
KO23a
]
).
It is known that for each
Dynkin quiver
Q
, there exists a unique Coxeter element
\tau_{Q}
all of whose reduced sequences are adapted to
Q
and form a single commutation equivalent class. Conversely, for any Coxeter element
\tau
, there exists a unique Dynkin quiver
Q
such that
\tau=\tau_{Q}
.

For each Dynkin quiver
Q
, it is also known that there exists a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
, which is adapted to
Q
. Furthermore, the set of all
reduced sequences adapted to
Q
makes a single commutation class of
w_{\circ}
, denoted by
[Q]
. Note that
[Q]\neq[Q^{\prime}]

unless there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that
\xi_{i}-\xi^{\prime}_{i}=k
for all
i\in\triangle_{0}
, where
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}

are the height functions of
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, respectively. For a Dynkin quiver
Q
with a source
i
, we have

(3.1)

\displaystyle[s_{i}Q]=r_{i}[Q].

3.2.
Repetition quiver and
Q
-coordinates

Let us recall the repetition quiver and related notions:

Definition 3.2
.

(
[
FHOO22
,
KO23a
,
JLO23
]
)

(1)

We define the
repetition quiver

{\widehat{\triangle}}=({\widehat{\triangle}}_{0},{\widehat{\triangle}}_{1})
associated to
\triangle
as follows:

{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
is as in (
2.6
) and

\displaystyle{\widehat{\triangle}}_{1}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,p)\xrightarrow{\,\;-\langle h_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j}\rangle\;\,}(j,p+1)\mid(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0},\ d(i,j)=1\}.

Here
(i,p)\xrightarrow{\,\;-\langle h_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j}\rangle\;\,}(j,p+1)
denotes
(-\langle h_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j}\rangle)
-many arrows from
(i,p)
to
(j,p+1)
.

(2)

A subset
\mathcal{R}\subset{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
is said to be
convex
if it satisfies the following condition: For any oriented path
(x_{1}\to x_{2}\to\cdots\to x_{l})
consisting of arrows
in
{\widehat{\triangle}}
, we have
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{l}\}\subset\mathcal{R}
if and only if
\{x_{1},x_{l}\}\subset\mathcal{R}
.

(3)

For a height function
\xi
on
\triangle
, set

(3.2)

\displaystyle{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\ |\ p\leqslant\xi_{i}\}.

Note that
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
is a convex subset of
{\widehat{\triangle}}
for any height function
\xi
.

.

{\widehat{\triangle}}
’s are depicted as follows:

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}

In what follows, we assign a coordinate system to
{\widehat{\triangle}}
arising from a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
. For each
i\in\triangle_{0}
, define

\gamma_{i}^{Q}\mathbin{:=}(1-\tau_{Q})\varpi_{i}\in\Phi_{+}.

There exists a bijection
\phi_{Q}:{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\to\Phi_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
defined recursively as follows
[
HL15
,
FO21
,
KO23a
]
:

(i)

\phi_{Q}(i,\xi_{i})=(\gamma_{i}^{Q},0)
for each
i\in I
.

(ii)

If
\phi_{Q}(i,p)=({\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k)
, we have

\phi_{Q}(i,p\pm 2)=\begin{cases}(\tau_{Q}^{\mp}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k)&\text{ if }\tau_{Q}^{\mp}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi_{+},\\
(-\tau_{Q}^{\mp}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k\pm 1)&\text{ if }\tau_{Q}^{\mp}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi_{-}.\end{cases}

We call
\phi_{Q}
the
Q
-coordinate of
{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

For
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
with
\phi_{Q}(i,p)=({\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k)
, we set

\pi_{Q}(i,p)\mathbin{:=}(-1)^{k}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}\in\Phi.

Lemma 3.4
.

(
[
FO21
, (3.18)]
,
[
KO23a
, Lemma 3.11]
)
.
For a Dynkin quiver
Q
and
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, we have

\pi_{Q}(i,p)=\tau_{Q}^{(\xi_{i}-p)/2}\gamma_{i}^{Q}.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
HL15
,
FO21
,
KO23a
]
)
.

Let
(i,p),(j,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
and
Q
be any Dynkin quiver. Then we have

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k+l+\delta(p\geqslant s)}\delta((i,p)\neq(j,s))({\mspace{1mu}\alpha},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}),

where
\phi_{Q}(i,p)=({\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k)
and
\phi_{Q}(j,s)=({\mspace{1mu}\beta},l)
.

3.3.
Q
-weights, block decomposition of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
and AR-quivers

Based on Theorem
3.5
, for an
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial
\widetilde{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{X}
-monomial
m
), we define

{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m})=\sum_{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})\pi_{Q}(i,p)\quad\text{ (resp.~}{\rm wt}_{Q}(m)=\sum_{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}u_{i,p}(m)\pi_{Q}(i,p))

and call it
the
Q
-weight of
\widetilde{m}

(
resp.
m)
.
Note that
{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m}*\widetilde{m}^{\prime})={\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m})+{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})
for
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomials
\widetilde{m}
and
\widetilde{m}^{\prime}
.

Corollary 3.6
.

(
[
FO21
, (5.3)]
,
[
KO23a
, Corollary 5.6]
)
.

For any
(i,p)\in I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
with
(i,p-1)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
and a Dynkin quiver
Q
, we have

{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{B}_{i,p})={\rm wt}_{Q}(B_{i,p})=0.

An element
\widetilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
is said to be
homogeneous
if

{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m})={\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\quad\text{ for any monomials $\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}$ in $\widetilde{x}$ and any Dynkin quiver $Q$.}

In this case, we set
{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{x})\mathbin{:=}{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{m})
for any monomial
\widetilde{m}
in
\widetilde{x}
.
By Theorem
2.7
, Theorem
2.8
, and Corollary
3.6
, the elements
F_{q}(m)
and
L_{q}(m)
are homogeneous and their
Q
-weights are the same as
{\rm wt}_{Q}(m)
. The proposition below is proved in
[
CM05
,
KKOP22
,
FO21
]
when
Q
is of simply-laced type, in the context of blocks of category.

Proposition 3.7
.

For any Dynkin quiver
Q
, we have a natural decomposition of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
with respect to
\mathsf{Q}
:

\mathfrak{K}_{q}=\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\bigoplus$}}\limits_{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}}\mathfrak{K}^{Q}_{q}[{\mspace{1mu}\beta}],

where

\mathfrak{K}^{Q}_{q}[{\mspace{1mu}\beta}]=\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\bigoplus$}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathcal{M}_{+},\\
{\rm wt}_{Q}(m)={\mspace{1mu}\beta}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]F_{q}(m)=\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\bigoplus$}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathcal{M}_{+},\\
{\rm wt}_{Q}(m)={\mspace{1mu}\beta}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]L_{q}(m)=\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\bigoplus$}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathcal{M}_{+},\\
{\rm wt}_{Q}(m)={\mspace{1mu}\beta}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]E_{q}(m).

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\widetilde{x},\widetilde{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
be homogeneous elements whose ranges are
[a,b]
and
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
, respectively. Assume that either
a<b<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}
or
a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}<a<b
. Then we have

\widetilde{x}*\widetilde{x}^{\prime}=(-1)^{\delta(b^{\prime}<a)}q^{({\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{x}),{\rm wt}_{Q}(\widetilde{x}^{\prime}))}\widetilde{x}^{\prime}*\widetilde{x}

for any Dynkin quiver
Q
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Theorem
3.5
.
∎

For a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, let

\Gamma^{Q}_{0}\mathbin{:=}\phi_{Q}^{-1}(\Phi_{+}\times\{0\}).

Then
\Gamma^{Q}_{0}
is a convex subset of
{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
. We set

\Gamma^{Q}\mathbin{:=}{}^{\Gamma^{Q}_{0}}{\widehat{\triangle}},

where we understand
\Gamma^{Q}
as a sub-quiver of
{\widehat{\triangle}}
with
\Gamma^{Q}_{0}
as its vertex set and the arrows induced from
{\widehat{\triangle}}
, and call it
the
(
combinatorial
)
AR-quiver of
Q
.
Note that the vertices of
\Gamma^{Q}
can be labeled by
\Phi_{+}
via
\phi_{Q}
. Thus, we often identify
\Gamma^{Q}_{0}
with
\Phi_{+}
.

Example 3.9
.

(3.3)
